Shri. K.Y. Modgekar, Ld. Advocate for the second party. 
AWARD 
(Dated : 03/04/2025) 


1. The present reference is filed under the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947, for adjudication of the dispute between 
the parties who are referred as first party (the employer) and second party (the employee) accordingly. 


2. The case of the second party — workman as per his statement of claim Exh. U-1! is that the first party i.e. the 
Central Bank of India is a nationalized bank with hundreds of branches across the country and having thousands of 
employees working in it. There are independent Service Rules for the employees of the first party Bank. As per the 
said Service Rules, the first party Bank is required to allow permanency to the workers who had worked for 60 days, 
and at present, the workers who have worked for 45 days, must be allowed permanency. The work of Sweeper is 
essential and that of permanent nature. It is further contended by the second party that he worked as a Sweeper in 
Currency Chest Branch since April-2005 on daily wages. He was initially paid Rs. 30/- on vouchers. Appointment 
letter and pay slips were not given to the daily wage workers. The second party was illegally terminated w.e.f. 
01.08.2017. His entire service was continuous and unblemished. He is eligible for permanency in service as per the 
Service Rules. 


3. It is further contended by the second party that advertisements are published from time to time by the first 
party Bank for recruitment. Temporary and daily wage employees of the first party bank were only required to apply 
at the time of these advertisements. Temporary and casual employees are also entitled to apply for permanent 
appointment, if the concerned employees possess the requisite qualification i.e. caste category. The first party bank 
has permanent staff of Sweepers and, pay, leave, vacations and other benefits are provided as per Service Rules to 
them. However, daily wage workers and temporary workers are paid nominal wages. There is no difference between 
the work of permanent Sweeper and daily wage Sweeper. 


4. It is further contended by the second party workman that his entire service from 2005 to 2017 is continuous 
and clean. He has rendered 240 of continuous service in the preceding 12 months prior to his oral termination dated 
01.08.2017. Show cause notice or charge sheet was not issued to the second party workman. Departmental enquiry 
was also not conducted against him. Seniority list was also not published by the first party. His juniors are still in 
service with the first party. His last pay was Rs. 50/- per day. The first party paid his wages on vouchers during his 
service tenure. He was terminated illegally without following due process of law. 


Ds It is further contended by the second party workman that workers are not paid equal pay for equal work. 
After his termination, the second party workman failed to get the employment, despite several attempts for getting 
employment. Hence, it is prayed to declare the oral termination of the second party dated 01.08.2017 as illegal, 
improper, contrary to principles of natural justice, and direct the first party to reinstate him in service along with 
continuity and full back wages. It is also contended by the second party that he filed Reference (I.T.) No. 06 of 2018 
for permanency. 


6. The first party appeared and resisted the statement of claim by filing written statement at Exh. C-2. All the 
contentions of the second party in the statement of claim are denied by the first party. It is contended by the first party 
that the statement of claim is not true and correct. No cause of action has arisen for filing the present dispute. There is 
no employer-employee relationship between the first party and the second party. The second party has never worked 
as a temporary or permanent worker with the first party. Recruitment of all the posts of the first party is done through 
advertisement and examination by following due procedure of recruitment. There is no Service Rules regarding 
permanency of a workman who served / worked continuously for 60 days or 45 days. The first party publishes regular 
advertisement for recruitment of employees as per requirement. The said advertisement mentions the qualification for 
each post, and any eligible person can apply for that post as per the procedure given in the advertisement. Candidates 
who qualify in the examination are recruited as per the merit list. The second party was called only as and when the 
work was available. The second party has also filed Reference (I.T.) No. 06 of 2018 for the same dispute as involved 
in the present Reference. Thus, the present Reference is not maintainable. Hence, claim of the second party deserves 
to be rejected. Accordingly, the first party prayed to answer the reference in negative. 


7. The issues along with findings thereon and the reasons therefor are as under :- 
Sr. No. sss Findings 
1 Whether the Reference is maintainable in view of Reference (I.T.) No. 06 of 
2018? 1 


[भाग 1---खण्ड 3(ii)] भारत का राजपत्र : फरवरी 7, 2026/माध 18, 1947 585 


2 Does the second party (workman) prove that he has performed continuous 
service as a Sweeper with the first party for 240 days in a year, from the date Yes 
of his appointment till the date of his oral termination on 01.08.2017, as ; 
claimed? 
2 Does the second party prove that the action of first party in orally terminating 
his service from 01.08.2017 is illegal and against the principles of natural Yes. 
justice? 
3 Whether the second party (workman) is entitled to the relief of declaration to Yes 
that effect? हि 
4 Whether the second party is entitled to the reinstatement in service along with No. But, entitled for 
continuity and full back wages? compensation. 
5 What order? As per final Award. 
REASONS 
As to issue No. 1 :- 
8. The first party has raised the issue of maintainability on the ground that the second party has filed Reference 


(.T.) No. 06 of 2018 before the Hon’ble Industrial Court for the same dispute as is involved in the present Reference. 
In this regard, the second party has fairly pleaded in his statement of claim that he has filed the Reference (I.T.) No. 
06 of 2018 for permanency. It is well settled position of law that jurisdiction to adjudicate the matters in respect of 
permanency lies with the Hon’ble Industrial Court and not with this Court. The Reference (I.T.) No. 06 of 2018, itself 
reflects that the dispute relating to permanency is referred to the Hon’ble Industrial Tribunal. Moreover, in the present 
Reference, the dispute involved is in respect of the legality of oral termination of the second party, which definitely 
comes under the jurisdiction of this Court. Having regards to all these facts and circumstances of the case, this Court 
is of the considered view that this Court has jurisdiction to entertain and decide the present dispute related with oral 
termination of the second party. Thus, the present Reference is maintainable before this Court. Hence, issue No. 1 
answered in affirmative. 


As to issue Nos. 2 to 5:- 


9. In support of his contentions, the second party workman has filed his affidavit in lieu of examination-in-chief 
at Exh. U-9, and has reiterated all the contentions of his statement of claim. The second party has filed documents on 
record along with lists of documents at Exh. U-14, U-17 and U-19. The documents are also filed along with 
inspection report Exh. U-8. 


10. On the contrary, the first party has filed affidavit in lieu of examination-in-chief of Mr. Adhiraj Avinash Gote, 
Branch Manager of the first party at Exh. C-6. 

11. I have also gone through the document filed along with the list at Exh. U-2 i.e. letter of the Regional Labour 
Commissioner, Pune. 

12. The facts admitted by the second party in his cross-examination are as under :- 

1) In the year 2005, the first party has not published any advertisement for recruitment. 

2) Appointment letter was not given to him in the year, 2005. There is no written agreement of service in 


between the first party and himself. 
3) He was working on daily wage basis with the first party. 


4) At the time of recruitment after advertisement, exemption / facility is given to the daily wage workers. 
However, daily wage workers were required to apply as per the advertisement, and after conducting written and oral 
tests, the eligible candidates were recruited. 


5) The first party bank had published an advertisement in the year 2013. (He is unable to state whether the said 
advertisement got cancelled or not.) 

6) Daily wage workers have no right to sign on muster-roll. 

7) He had no right to perform written work in record of the first party bank. 

13. The facts admitted by the witness of the first party Mr. Adhiraj Avinash Gote, Branch Manager in his cross- 


examination are as under :- 


1) He was not attached to Shrirampur Branch during the working period of the second party. 


586 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : FEBRUARY 7, 2026/MAGHA 18, 1947 [Part II—SEC. 3(ii)] 


2) They have paid wages to workmen on vouchers. Entry of vouchers is taken in cash book and ledger book. 


3) Juniors of the second party workman Mr. Siddharth Mohan and Mr. Mehetre were working after discounting 
of the second party. 


4) After discontinuing the workman, Mr. Jogdand is working on daily wages. 
5) The work of cleanliness is required to be done in the first party bank. 
14. Having regard to all these oral as well as documentary evidence on record, and after perusal of Statement of 


Claim and Written Statement, the factual position of the case is summarized as under :- 


1) The second party workman has worked on daily wages basis with the first party bank for a period from 
April-2005 to 01.08.2017. 


2) The second party workman was not appointed by any written order or by adopting recruitment process. 

3) The second party workman had no right to sign on muster-roll. 

4) Payment (wages) of the second party workman was drawn on vouchers. 

5) Lastly, the Second party workman was getting Rs. 50/- per day as wages. 

6) Vouchers are destroyed after 10 years. 

7) Entries of vouchers are available in cash books. 

8) Work of cleanliness in the first party bank is of permanent nature. 

9) The second party workman has been terminated orally without issuing any notice or charge-sheet or without 


conducting any enquiry. 


15. The witness of the first party Mr. Adhiraj Avinash has admitted in his cross-examination that the entries of 
vouchers are available in the Cash books. In this situation, the first party could have filed copies of the Cash Books on 
record to show that the second party has or has not worked 240 days in 12 months preceding his termination. The first 
party has failed to file any document on record to show that the second party has worked for less than 240 days in 
preceding 12 months of his termination. 


16. Learned Advocate on behalf of the second party workman argued that the first party has not filed relevant 
documents in its custody, and hence, adverse inference may be drawn against the second party. In support of his 
arguments, learned Advocate for the second party relied upon the following case laws :- 


1) M.P. Electricity Board, Vidisha V/s. Hariram and another, reported in 2000 (87) FLR 750. In the said case, 
the Hon’ble Madhya Pradesh High Court observed that one cannot also be oblivious of the fact that in matters 
pertaining to such engagement on daily wages, the record is normally maintained by the employer and, in view of the 
categorical assertion of the employees that they had worked continuously during the period in question and the 
defence that they had been only intermittently engaged, for non-production of the Muster Rolls an adverse inference 
could be drawn against the employer. 


2) Bright Export Limited v/s. Central Board of Trustee, EPF Organisation, reported in 2016 LLR 487. In the 
said case, the Hon’ble Delhi High Court observed as under :- 


“When a particular fact is within the special knowledge of the opposite party, the burden to prove the 
same lies upon the opposite party in terms of section 106 of the Indian Evidence Act, 1872, even if the 
same has been pleaded by other party.” 


3) Sanjay Kumar s/o Surendra Kumar Sharma V/s. Chief Executive Officer, Janpad Panchayat, Ratlam, 
reported in 2010 LLR 1065. In the said case, the Hon’ble Madhya Pradesh High Court observed as under :- 


“If the party possesses best evidence and is not produced before the Court, an adverse inference can be drawn 
under the Evidence Act against such a party.” 


In view of the observations of the Hon’ble High Court in the cases of M.P. Electricity Board, Vidisha 
(supra), Bright Export Limited (supra), and Sanjay Kumar s/o Surendra Kumar Sharma (supra), it becomes clear 
that adverse inference can be drawn against the party who despite possessing evidence, fails to produce the same. 


17. The copies of the Cash Book are expected to be in custody of the first party, and it was for the first party to 
produce the same. In such circumstances, non-production of the Cash Books by the first party leads to drawn adverse 
inference that if the same had been produced, it would have supported to the contentions of the second party. In cross- 
examination of the second party, nothing is come on record to disbelieve the contentions of the second party that he 
was worked for more than 240 days in 12 months preceding his oral termination. Moreover, there is no contentions of 
the first party that it is ready to get the second party resumed on duty. Therefore, contentions of the second party that 
he was orally terminated, can be accepted as proved. 


[भाग --खण्ड 3(ii)] भारत का राजपत्र : फरवरी 7, 2026/माध 18, 1947 587 


18. It is also contention of the second party workman that the daily wage workers who had worked for more than 
65 or 45 days, were made permanent in service. However, there is nothing on record to establish the said alleged fact. 
The second party failed to prove the said alleged fact by adducing oral or documentary evidence. Moreover, the 
present case is not the case for deciding permanency in service. 


19. Nothing is available to disclose that recruitment process was conducted by the first party after termination of 
the second party, and the second party was not given opportunity to participate in such a recruitment process. 
Moreover, nothing is available to disclose that the second party participated in any recruitment process. 


20. Now, turning towards the legal aspects involved in the case, it is necessary to reproduce relevant Section of 
the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947, which is as under :- 


“Section 25-F. 
Conditions precedent to retrenchment of workmen :- 


No workman employed in any industry who has been in continuous service for not less than one 
year under an employer shall be retrenched by that employer until - 


(a) the workman has been given one month's notice in writing indicating the reasons for retrenchment 
and the period of notice has expired, or the workman has been paid in lieu of such notice, wages for the 
period of the notice. 


(b) the workman has been paid, at the time of retrenchment, compensation which shall be equivalent to 
fifteen days average pay for every completed year of continuous service or any part thereof in excess of six 
months; and 


(c) notice in the prescribed manner is served on the appropriate Government or such authority as may 
be specified by the appropriate Government by notification in the Official Gazette.” 


21. Learned Advocate on behalf of the second party workman has relied upon the following case laws :- 


1) H.D. Singh V/s. Reserve Bank of India and others, Civil Appeal No. 6417/NL/1983 dated 10.09.1985. In 
the said case, the Hon’ble Supreme Court has observed that the striking off the name of the appellant from List II 
amounted to retrenchment under Section 2(00) of the Act and was in violation of Section 25-F. 


2) Umesh Saxena V/s. Presiding Officer, Labour Court, Agra and others, reported in 1993 FLR (66) 566. In 
the said case, the Hon’ble Allahabad High Court has observed that retrenchment without following the mandate 
contained in Section 25-F of the Industrial Disputes Act is void and cannot be supported in law. 


3) General Manager, Telecom, Nagpur and others V/s. Naresh Brijlal Charote, reported in 2001 LAB 1.८. 
2127. In the said case, the Hon’ble Bombay High Court has observed as under :- 


“Tt has been time and again held by this Court as well as by the Apex Court that the non-compliance of the 
mandatory provisions of Section 25F of the Act would render the termination of service void ab initio.” 


4) K.V. Durga Prasad & Ors. V/s. Sri. Durgamalleshwara Swami Vari Devasthanam Vijayawada & Ors., 
reported in 1996 LLR 329. In the said case, the Hon’ble Andhra Preadesh High Court has observed as under :- 


---------- . Thus, I find that the impugned termination orders are in violation of principles of natural 
justice. Without going to the respective merits of the case, I am inclined to allow this writ petition on the 
ground of contravention of principles of natural justice.” 


5) Jaggo V/s. Union of India and others, reported in 2025 (1) Bom.L.C. 261 (SC). In the said case, 
the Hon’ble Supreme Court has observed that the claim by the respondents that these were not regular posts lacks 
merit, as the nature of the work performed by the appellants was perennial and fundamental to the functioning of the 
offices. The Hon’ble Supreme Court also observed that the termination letters, issued without prior notice or 
explanation, violated fundamental principles of natural justice. 


6) Gauri Shankar Vs. State of Rajasthan, reported in 2015 LLR 7885. In the said case, the Hon’ble Supreme 
Court has observed that non-compliance of the mandatory requirements under Sections 25F, 25G and 25H of the Act 
by the respondent-Department rendered its action of termination of the services of the workman as void ab initio in 
law. 


In view of the observations of the Hon’ble in the cases of H.D. Singh (supra), Umesh Saxena (supra), 
General Manager, Telecom, Nagpur (supra), K.V. Durga Prasad (supra), Jaggo (supra), and Gauri Shankar (supra), 
it becomes clear that retrenchment without complying provisions of Section 25-F and the principles of natural justice 
is void. 


22: Learned Advocate on behalf of the second party workman argued that juniors of the second party are still in 
service with first party, and on the basis of ‘last come first go’ rule, the first party should have to terminate / retrench 


588 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : FEBRUARY 7, 2026/MAGHA 18, 1947 [PART II—SECc. 3(ii)] 


juniors of the second party before terminating him. In support of his arguments, learned Advocate for the second party 
relied upon the following case laws :- 


1) Samishta Dube V/s. City Board, Etawah & Another, reported in 1999 CLR 460. In the said case, the 
Hon’ble Supreme Court observed as under :- 


“9. It is true that the rule of ‘first come, last go' in section 6-P could be deviated from by an employer because 
the section uses the word ‘ordinarily’. It is, therefore, permissible for the employer to deviate from the rule in 
cases of lack of efficiency or loss of confidence etc., as held in M/s. Swadesamitran Limited, Madras v. Their 
Workmen, [1960] 3 SCR 144. But the burden will then be on the employer to justify the deviation. No such 
attempt has been made in the present case. Hence, it is clear that there is clear violation of Section 6-P of the 
U.P. Act.” 


2) Central Welfare Board and Others V/s. Ms. Anjali Bepari and Others, reported in 1996 LLR 
1089. In the said case, the Hon’ble Supreme Court observed that, “dispensing with the services should be on last- 
come-first-go basis, i.e., the junior-most incumbent has to go out first.” 


3) Trade-Wings Limited V/s. Prabhakar Dattararam Phodkar of Bombay and Ors, reported in 1992 LR (1) 
480. In the said case, the Hon’ble Bombay High Court observed that Rule 81 of the Industrial Disputes (Bombay) 
Rules enjoins upon the employer before effecting retrenchment to prepare a list of workmen in the particular category 
from which retrenchment is contemplated arranged according to the seniority or their service in that category. The 
object of the Rule is apparent. It is to ensure the due compliance of Section 250 i.e. last come 
first go. 


In the cases of Samishta Dube (supra), Central Welfare Board (supra) and Trade-Wings Limited (supra), the 
principle of “First Come Last Go” was emphasized. 


23. Learned Advocate for the second party relied upon the following case laws :- 


1) Vilas Agaji Pawar and others V/s. The Union of India Additional Solicitor General and Others, Writ 
Petition No. 379 of 2024 dated 12" January, 2024. In the said case, the Hon’ble Bombay High Court observed as 
under :- 


“19. It is, thus, crystal clear that the OTM was meant only for those Safai Karmacharis or Sub-Staff, who were 
already working with the Bank. These directions of the Honourable Supreme Court read with the law laid 
down in Umadevi (supra), lead to a sine-qua-non that the only option available for the Bank was to ensure 
that the long serving employees as like the Petitioners at it's various establishments and branches in India, 
were considered for the OTM.” 


2) Spentex Industrial Limited V/s. Member, Industrial Court, Nagpur and others, reported in 2011 (131) 
FLR 843. In the said case, the Hon’ble Bombay High Court Bench at Nagpur observed as under :- 


“Though, it is debatable whether the principles applicable in respect of Section 33 of the Industrial Disputes 
Act, would stricto sensu apply to the proceedings under the M.R.T.U. & P.U.L.P. Act, 1971, but the least that 
was expected from the petitioner-Company was that when the parties were before the Court and when an 
application for interim relief was pending, the petitioner-Company ought to have informed the Court of the 
action it proposes to take based on the enquiry officer's report. Having not done so, in my view, the Courts 
below were right in protecting the services of the respondent No. 3 pending the Complaint. The facts of the 
present case are such that the discretion exercised by the Courts below in granting the interim relief cannot be 


faulted with.” 


Facts of the cases of Vilas Agaji Pawar (supra) and Spentex Industrial Limited (supra), are not similar with 
the facts of the instant case. Because, in the instant case, nothing is to show that long service of employees were not 
considered for OTM. Moreover, in the instant case, the application for any interim relief is not subjudiced. 


24. Learned Advocate on behalf of the first party bank has relied upon the case of Secretary, State of Karnataka 
and Ors. V/s. Umadevi and others, reported in AIR 2006 SC 1806. In the said case, the Hon’ble Supreme Court has 
observed as under:- 


-------- . It has also led to persons who get employed, without the following of a regular procedure or 
even through the backdoor or on daily wages, approaching the Courts, seeking directions to make them 
permanent in their posts and to prevent to regular recruitment to the concerned posts. - - - - - - - - - . It is time, 
that Courts desist from issuing orders preventing regular selection or recruitment at the instance of such 
persons and from issuing directions for continuance of those who have not secured regular appointments as per 
procedure established. The passing of orders for continuance, tends to defeat the very Constitutional scheme of 
public employment. - - - - - - - - - a 


[भाग [Ives 3(ii)] भारत का राजपत्र : फरवरी 7, 2026/माध 18, 1947 589 


20% Learned Advocate on behalf of the second party has relied upon the case of Chief Conservator of Forests, 
Pune (T) and another, V/s. Janabai Sonaba Sarpale, reported in 2019 (1) Bom.LC 18. In the said case, the Hon’ble 
Bombay High Court has observed as under :- 


“25. The Apex Court in MSRTC (supra) has clearly observed that the issue relating to unfair labour practices 
was neither referred nor considered or decided in Uma Devi's case and therefore, the ruling in., Uma Devi 
(supra) does not denude the Industrial and Labour Courts of their statutory power under section 30 read with 
section 32 of the MRTUP and PULP Act to order permanency of the workers who have been victim of unfair 
labour practice on the part of the employer under Item 6 of Schedule IV. The Apex Court in fact, held that Uma 
Devi (supra) cannot be held to have overridden the powers of the Industrial and Labour Courts in passing 
appropriate order under section 30 of the MRTU and PULP Act, once a case of unfair labour practice on the 
part of the employer under Item 6 of Schedule IV is established.” 


In the instant case, this Court is not dealing with the matter of permanency. Thus, facts of the case 
of Umadevi (supra) and the case of Chief Conservator of Forests (supra) are different from the facts of the instant 
case. 


26. The learned Advocate for the second party relied upon the case of Jayantibhai Raojibhai Patel V/s. 
Municipal Council, Narkhed, Civil Appeal No. 6188 of 2019, arising out of SLP (C) No. 8112 of 2019, dated 
21.08.2019. In the said case, the Hon’ble Supreme Court has observed as under :- 


“13. Having due regard to the principles which have been enunciated in Deepali Surwase by this Court, the 
High Court was not, in our view, justified in denying the back-wages to the appellant altogether. Bearing in 
mind the circumstances which have been noted above, a lumpsum compensation should be directed to be 
paid.” 


27. The, the Hon’ble Bombay High Court in the case of Divisional Secretary, Maharashtra State Board of 
Secondary and Secondary Education, Nagpur and another V/s. Mohd. Naim s/o Abdul Rahim, reported in 2009 (1) 
Bom. LC 453 (Bom), has made it clear by observing that “Jt therefore follows that the mode or manner of entry into 
service is one of the relevant factors to be considered here and distinction between cases of prayer for regularisation 
& reinstatement being made earlier is now not available after the constitutional bench verdict of Hon. Apex Court in 
case of Secretary, State of Karnataka .vrs. Umadevi (supra). In public employment therefore mere violation of S. 25F 
or S.25G or S.25H by itself may not be sufficient to sustain reinstatement if employment is not secured in accordance 
with relevant rules for recruitment or Art. 14 of the Constitution Of India.” 


28. Having regard to all the legal as well as factual aspects involved in the matter as discussed herein above, and 
after giving thoughtful consideration to the observations of the Hon’ble Supreme Court and the Hon’ble High Court 
in the above referred case laws, this Court is of the view that the provisions of the Industrial Dispute Act are 
applicable to the first party, and the second party has completed 240 days in the preceding 12 months of his oral 
termination dated 01.08.2017, and his termination is without following mandatory provisions of the Industrial 
Disputes Act, 1947, which is illegal and improper termination violating the statutory provisions of the Industrial 
Disputes Act, 1947. Accordingly, the second party is entitled for declaration of the same. Resultantly, issue Nos. 1 to 3 
are answered affirmative. 


29. Considering the entire factual and admitted position of the present case as well as the provisions of law, 
reinstatement of the second party workman with the first party will not be appropriate. Instead of reinstatement, 
awarding a reasonable lump-sum amount of compensation to the second party workman would be just and 
appropriate. According to the second party, his last pay was Rs. 50/- per day. In view of the facts and circumstances 
of the case, taking into consideration last designation, last paid wages, service tenure and age of the second party 
workman, in my considered view, awarding lump-sum compensation of an amount which will be 1000 times of his 
last per day wages i.e. 1000 x 50 = 50,000/- would be just and appropriate. Thus, awarding lump-sum compensation 
of the amount of Rs. 50,000/- to the second party workman would meet the ends of justice. Resultantly, issue No. 4 is 
answered accordingly. For issue No. 5, the following Award is passed. 


AWARD 
1. The reference is answered partly affirmative as under :- 
A. It is declared that the oral termination of the second party dated 01.08.2017 is illegal and improper. 


B. The second party workman is entitled for lump-sum compensation amount of Rs. 50,000/- (Rs. Fifty Thousand 
Only). 


C. The first party is directed to pay compensation amount of Rs. 50,000/- (Rs. Fifty Thousand Only) to the second 
party workman. 


590 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : FEBRUARY 7, 2026/MAGHA 18, 1947 [PART II—SEC. 3(ii)] 


D. The first party is hereby directed to comply the order within two months from the date of publication of the 
Award. 


E. If the first party failed to comply the order within time, then the second party workman is entitled to simple 
interest at the rate of 6% per annum on the compensation amount of Rs. 50,000/- (Rs. Fifty Thousand Only), from 
the date of this Award till its actual realization. 


2. Copies of this Award be sent to Regional Labour Commissioner, office of the Regional Labour Commissioner, 1, 
Kaul Building, Shankar Seth Road, Gururnanak Nagar, Pune — 411002 for publication and further necessary 
action. 


3. No order as to costs. 
A.G. DESHMUKH, Presiding Officer & Judge,

AWARD 
(Dated : 31/08/2024) 


1. The present reference is filed under the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947, for adjudication of the dispute between 
the parties who are referred as first party (the employer) and second party (the employee) accordingly. 


Qi The case of the second party — workman as per his statement of claim Exh. U-1! is that the first party i.e. the 
Central Bank of India is a nationalized bank with hundreds of branches across the country and having thousands of 
employees working in it. There are independent Service Rules for the employees of the first party Bank. As per the 
said Service Rules, the first party Bank is required to allow permanency to the workers who had worked for 60 days, 
and at present, the workers who have worked for 45 days, must be allowed permanency. The work of Sweeper is 
essential and that of permanent nature. It is further contended by the second party that he worked as a Sweeper in 
Samsherpur Branch since January-2007 on daily wages. He was initially paid Rs. 50/- on vouchers. Appointment 
letter and pay slips were not given to the daily wage workers. The second party was illegally terminated 
w.e.f. 01.02.2016. His entire service was continuous and clean. He is eligible for permanency in service as per the 
Service Rules. 


3. It is further contended by the second party that advertisements are published from time to time by the first 
party Bank for recruitment. Temporary and daily wage employees of the first party bank were only required to apply 
at the time of these advertisements. Temporary and casual employees are also entitled to apply for permanent 
appointment, if the concerned employees possess the requisite qualification i.e. caste category. The first party bank 
has permanent staff of Sweepers and, pay, leave, vacations and other benefits are provided as per Service Rules to 
them. However, daily wage workers and temporary workers are paid nominal wages. There is no difference between 
the work of permanent Sweeper and daily wage Sweeper. 


4. It is further contended by the second party workman that, his entire service from 2007 to 01.02.2016 is 
continuous and clean. He has rendered 240 of continuous service in the preceding 12 months prior to his oral 
termination dated 01.02.2016. Show cause notice or charge sheet was not issued to the second party workman. 
Departmental enquiry was also not conducted against him. Seniority list was also not published by the first party. His 
juniors are still in service with the first party. His last pay is Rs. 100/- per day. The first party paid his wages on 
vouchers during his service tenure. He was terminated illegally without following due process of law. 


5. It is further contended by the second party workman that workers are not paid equal pay for equal work. 
After his termination, the second party workman failed to get the employment, despite several attempts for getting 
employment. Hence, it is prayed to declare the oral termination of the second party dated 01.02.2016 as illegal, 
improper, contrary to natural justice, and direct the first party to reinstate him in service along with continuity and full 
back wages. 


6. The first party appeared and resisted the statement of claim by filing written statement at Exh. C-5. All the 
contentions of the second party in the statement of claim are denied by the first party. It is contended by the first party 
that the statement of claim is not true and correct. No cause of action has arisen for filing the present dispute. There is 
no employer-employee relationship between the second party and the first party. The second party has never worked 
as a temporary or permanent worker with the first party. Recruitment of all the posts of the first party is done through 
advertisement and examination. There is no Service Rules regarding permanency of a workman who has served / 
worked for 60 days or 45 days. The first party publishes regular advertisement for recruitment of employees as per 
requirement. The said advertisement mentions the qualification for each post, and any eligible person can apply for 
that post as per the procedure given in the advertisement. Candidates who qualify in the examination are recruited as 
per the merit list. The second party has not participated in any recruitment process and has not been selected as its 
employee. The second party was called only as and when the work is available. The second party himself stopped 
doing work with the first party. Hence, claim of the second party deserves to be rejected. Accordingly, the first party 
prayed to answer the reference in negative. 


7. The issues framed by my learned Predecessor, along with the findings thereon and the reasons therefor are as 
under :- 


Sr. No. sts ईि॒िं॒ृिँंँंीाीण?.?.?ँ Findings 


592 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : FEBRUARY 7, 2026/MAGHA 18, 1947 [Part II—SEC. 3(ii)] 


1 Does the second party (workman) prove that the first party has illegally and 
improperly terminated his services vide order dated 01.02.2016, violating the Yes. 
statutory provisions of the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947? 


2 Whether the second party (workman) is entitled for the relief of reinstatement No. But, entitled for 
in service with full back wages and continuity of services w.e.f. 01.02.2016? compensation. 
3 What Award? As per final award. 
REASONS 


As to issue Nos. I to 3:- 


8. In support of his contentions, the second party workman has filed his affidavit of examination-in-chief at 
Exh. U-12, and has reiterated all the contentions of his statement of claim. The second party has filed documents on 
record along with lists of documents at Exh. U-14 viz; copies of the certificates issued by the first party dated 
24.11.2011, 28.01.2013, 28.01.2013, copies of vouchers dated 05.03.2015 and 01.02.2016, copy of correspondence 
letter dated 14.12.2016, copy of his Caste Certificate and Aadhar Card, at Exh. U-18 viz; copy of certificate issued by 
the first party dated 28.01.2013, correspondence letter dated 05.07.2021 and copy of Job Seeker Registration Slip, at 
Exh. U-21 viz; copy of his application submitted for recruitment of Safai Karmachari along with postal receipts, at 
Exh. U-20 viz; copy of agreement dated 09.08.2012, copies of two letters dated 14.08.2012, copy of Union’s letter 
dated 31.07.2023, at Exh. U-25 viz; copy of order dated 16.04.2024 in Special Leave to Appeal (C) No. 8130 / 2024 
of the Hon’ble Supreme Court. The second party has also filed total 17 documents on record along with lists at 
Exh. U-22. 


9; On the contrary, the first party has filed affidavits of examination-in-chief of Mr. Sagar Shivajirao Godase, 
Branch Manager of the first party at Exh. C-10, Ms. Sonali Jadhav, Manager at Exh. C-13, and have filed documents 
along with the list at Exh. C-12 viz; copy of Rules of Record Maintenance of the Bank (Updated up to Dec-2022). 


10. The facts admitted by the second party in his cross-examination are as under :- 
1) Before employing him, his interview was not conducted by the first party bank. 
2) Appointment letter was not given to him in the year, 2007. There is no written agreement of service in 


between the first party and himself. 
3) He was working on daily wage basis with the first party. 


4) At the time of the recruitment after advertisement, exemption / facility is given to the daily wage workers. 
However, daily wage workers were required to apply as per the advertisement, and after conducting written and oral 
tests, the eligible candidates were recruited. 


5) The first party bank had published an advertisement in the year 2013. (He cannot say whether the said 
advertisement got cancelled or not.) 

6) Daily wage workers have no right to sign on muster-roll. 

7) He was allotted the work as per availability. 

8) He had no right to perform written work in record of the first party bank. 

9) He was working as B.C.A. in Vakrangi Limited. 

11. The facts admitted by the witness of the first party Mr. Sagar Shivajirao Godase, Branch Manager in his 


cross-examination are as under :- 


1) The service tenure of the second party as daily wage worker was from January-2007 to December-2016. 
2) Details of vouchers cash payment are entered in to Cash Books and Cash Books are preserved permanently. 
3) At present, there are five employees working at his Branch including Branch Manager, Assistant Manager, 


Clerk, Head Cashier and Daptari or Sub-staff. 
4) In the bank, work of cleanliness was done by daily wage worker and it is not done by Daptari. 
5) The then Branch Manager can give the details about the work of the second party. 


12. The facts admitted by the witness of the first party Ms. Sonali Jadhav, Manager in her cross-examination are 


[भाग 1---खण्ड 3(ii)] भारत का राजपत्र : फरवरी 7, 2026/माध 18, 1947 593 


as under :- 


1) There is a voucher for every expenditure including details of the expenditure. Nature of work allotted to the 
second party is mentioned in the vouchers. 


2) Voucher is a single document having no copy, and it is in possession of the Bank. Entry of every voucher is 
made in to Cash Book. 


13. Having regard to all these oral as well as documentary evidence on record, and after perusal of Statement of 
Claim and Written Statement, the factual position of the case is summarized as under :- 


1) The second party workman has worked on daily wages basis with the first party bank for a period from 
January-2007 to 01.02.2016. 


2) The second party workman was not appointed by any written order or by adopting recruitment process. 

3) The second party workman had no right to sign on muster-roll. 

4) Payment (wages) of the second party workman was drawn on vouchers. 

5) Lastly, the Second party workman was getting Rs. 100/- per day as wages. 

6) Vouchers are destroyed after 10 years. 

7) Entries of vouchers are available in cash books. 

8) Work of cleanliness in the first party bank is of permanent nature. 

9) The second party workman has been terminated orally without issuing any notice or charge-sheet or without 


conducting any enquiry. 


14. In cross-examination of the second party, nothing is come on record to disbelieve the contentions of the 
second party that he was worked for more than 240 days in 12 months preceding his oral termination. Moreover, 
witness of the first party has admitted in his cross-examination that the entries of vouchers were available in the Cash 
books. In this situation, the first party could have filed copies of the Cash Books on record to show that the second 
party has worked or not for more than 240 days in 12 months preceding his termination. The first party has failed to 
file any document on record to show that the second party has not worked for more than 240 days in preceding 12 
months of his termination. The first party has only filed its Rules regarding maintenance of its record. 


15. The copies of the Cash Book are expected to be in custody of the first party, and it was for the first party to 
produce the same. In such circumstances, non-production of the Cash Books by the first party leads to drawn adverse 
inference that if the same had been produced, it would have supported to the contentions of the second party. 
Moreover, there is no contentions of the first party that it is ready to get the second party resumed on duty. Therefore, 
contentions of the second party that he was orally terminated, can be accepted as proved. 


16. It is also contention of the second party workman that the daily wage workers who had worked for more than 
65 or 45 days, were made permanent in service. However, there is noting on record to establish the said alleged fact. 
The second party failed to prove the said alleged fact by adducing oral or documentary evidence. 


17. It appears that the first party had conducted recruitment process during the period of daily wage service of 
the second party. However, nothing is available to disclose that the second party participated and got selected in the 
said recruitment process. Thus, it can not be kept out of consideration that the second party had opportunity to 
participate in the recruitment process through a regular mode. 


18. Now, turning towards the legal aspects involved in the case, it is necessary to reproduce relevant Section of 
the Industrial Disputes Act, 1947, which is as under :- 


“Section 25-F. 
Conditions precedent to retrenchment of workmen :- 


No workman employed in any industry who has been in continuous service for not less than one 
year under an employer shall be retrenched by that employer until - 


(a) the workman has been given one month's notice in writing indicating the reasons for retrenchment 
and the period of notice has expired, or the workman has been paid in lieu of such notice, wages for the 
period of the notice. 


594 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : FEBRUARY 7, 2026/MAGHA 18, 1947 [Part II—SEc. 3(ii)] 


(b) the workman has been paid, at the time of retrenchment, compensation which shall be equivalent to 
fifteen days average pay for every completed year of continuous service or any part thereof in excess of six 
months; and 


(c) notice in the prescribed manner is served on the appropriate Government or such authority as may 
be specified by the appropriate Government by notification in the Official Gazette.” 


19. Learned Advocate on behalf of the second party workman has relied upon the following case laws :- 


1) H.D. Singh V/s. Reserve Bank of India and _ others, Civil Appeal No. 6417/NL/1983 
dated 10.09.1985. In the said case, the Hon’ble Supreme Court observed as under :- 


“13. We hold that the appellant is entitled to succeed. We set aside the order of the Industrial Tribunal and hold 
that the striking off the name of the appellant from List IT amounted to retrenchment under Section 2(00) of the 
Act and was in violation of Section 25-F. We direct the first respondent-bank to enlist the appellant as a regular 
employee, as Tikke Mazdoor, to reinstate him and pay him his back wages up-to-date. The appeal is allowed 
with costs quantified at Rs. 3,000.” 


2) Trade-Wings Limited V/s. Prabhakar Dattararam Phodkar of Bombay and Ors, reported in 1992 LR (1) 
480. In the said case, the Hon’ble Bombay High Court observed as under :- 
“10. --------- . We have then the evidence of Madhav Narayan Nair, Administrative Officer of the 


petitioners. He has also deposed that the seniority list displayed on 11th February, 1985 was in respect of 
Junior Assistants in the Accounts Department. Since the list which was displayed was only in respect. of the 
Junior Assistants in the Accounts Department, the same would not comply with the mandatory requirement of 
Rule 81 of the Industrial Disputes (Bombay) Rules, 1957 and Section 25G of the Industrial Disputes Act. Rule 
81 of the Industrial Disputes (Bombay) Rules enjoins upon the employer before effecting retrenchment to 
prepare a list of workmen in the particular category from which retrenchment is contemplated arranged 
according to the seniority or their service in that category. The object of the Rule is apparent. It is to ensure the 
due compliance of Section 250 i.e. last come first go. In the present case what has been done is a display of 
seniority list only of the Accounts Department and not in respect of Junior Assistants of the other departments 
of the petitioner Company. The provisions of Rule 81 are mandatory and, therefore, breach of the same would 
render the retrenchment invalid. The order of retrenchment of Respondents No. I to 8 will, therefore, have to 
be quashed. 


11. There is yet another ground on which the impugned order of retrenchment deserves to be quashed. 
It is common ground that while effecting the impugned retrenchment, the petitioners have paid retrenchment 
compensation calculated on the basis of the monthly wages for 30 days and not 26 days. It is contended on 
behalf of the retrenched workers that this amounts to short payment which is equivalent to no payment and 
hence the impugned retrenchment is invalid as having been made with undue haste and not in good faith.” 


3) Umesh Saxena V/s. Presiding Officer, Labour Court, Agra and others, reported in 1993 FLR (66) 566. In 
the said case, the Hon’ble Allahabad High Court observed as under :- 


“11. Provisions of Section 25 of the Industrial Disputes Act were, therefore, necessarily to be followed by the 
employer before issuance of the order dated 16.7.82. The petitioner was retrenched and his retrenchment was 
made without following the mandate contained in Section 25-F of the Industrial Disputes Act. That being so, 
the retrenchment of the petitioner is void and cannot be supported in law.” 


4) Divisional Secretary, Maharashtra State Board of Secondary and Secondary Education, Nagpur and 
another V/s. Mohd. Naim s/o Abdul Rahim, reported in 2009 (1) Bom. LC 453 (Bom). In the said case, the Hon'ble 
Bombay High Court observed as under :- 


“11. It therefore follows that the mode or manner of entry into service is one of the relevant factors to be 
considered here and distinction between cases of prayer for regularisation & reinstatement being made earlier 
is now not available after the constitutional bench verdict of Hon. Apex Court in case of Secretary, State of 
Karnataka .vrs. Umadevi (supra). In public employment therefore mere violation of S. 25F or S.25G or S.25H 
by itself may not be sufficient to sustain reinstatement if employment is not secured in accordance with relevant 
rules for recruitment or Art. 14 of the Constitution Of India.” 


However, in the said case, it has also been observed that - 


“But then if it is proved by the complainant/employee that after terminating him in violation of S.25F or 
without publishing seniority list as required by the Rule 77 or 81 of Industrial Dispute Rules (Central or State), 
the work continued or his juniors were retained in service, and he could have performed work had he not been 
terminated, the grant of relief of reinstatement is justified. ”’ 


5) Jayantibhai Raojibhai Patel V/s. Municipal Council, Narkhed, Civil Appeal No. 6188 of 2019, arising out 
of SLP (C) No. 8112 of 2019, dated 21.08.2019. In the said case, the Hon’ble Supreme Court observed as under :- 


[भाग [Ives 3(ii)] भारत का राजपत्र : फरवरी 7, 2026/माध 18, 1947 595 


“12. In the present case the first inquiry resulted in a report which came to the conclusion that the charge of 
misconduct was not substantiated. Upon finding that the convening of a fresh inquiry without recording 
reasons was contrary to law, the High Court would have ordinarily granted liberty to the Municipal Council to 
take a fresh decision after due notice to the appellant. Such a course of action was, however, rendered 
impracticable by supervening events. The writ petition instituted by the appellant before the High Court in 
1996 remained pending for nearly eighteen years. The appellant had been removed from service on 29 June 
1996. Considering the lapse of time, reopening the proceedings would not be expedient in the interest of justice 
particularly when the appellant had, in the meantime, attained the age of superannuation in 2005. Relegating 
the appellant to a protracted course of action by restoring the proceedings before the disciplinary authority 
would also not be fair and proper after a lapse of nearly fourteen years since his retirement. 


13. Having due regard to the principles which have been enunciated in Deepali Surwase by this Court, the 
High Court was not, in our view, justified in denying the back-wages to the appellant altogether. Bearing in 
mind the circumstances which have been noted above, a lumpsum compensation should be directed to be 
paid.” 


6) Samishta Dube V/s. City Board, Etawah & Another, reported in 1999 CLR 460. In the said case, the 
Hon’ble Supreme Court observed as under :- 


“9. It is true that the rule of ‘first come, last go' in section 6-P could be deviated from by an employer because 
the section uses the word ‘ordinarily’. It is, therefore, permissible for the employer to deviate from the rule in 
cases of lack of efficiency or loss of confidence etc., as held in M/s. Swadesamitran Limited, Madras v. Their 
Workmen, [1960] 3 SCR 144. But the burden will then be on the employer to justify the deviation. No such 
attempt has been made in the present case. Hence, it is clear that there is clear violation of Section 6-P of the 
U.P. Act.” 


7) General Manager, Telecom, Nagpur and others V/s. Naresh Brijlal Charote, reported in 2001 LAB 1.८. 
2127. In the said case, the Hon’ble Bombay High Court observed as under :- 


“Now, if this test is applied to the present case, it can very well be said that respondent No. 1, who was 
appointed as a part-time sweeper and was required to do manual and unskilled work is a 'workman' within the 
meaning assigned to the said terms in the Act and as he worked for more than 240 days in a year, the 
provisions of Section 25F of the Act are applicable to the case in hand and as neither any notice, as 
contemplated under Section 25F of the Act, was served upon the respondent No. 1 nor he was paid 
compensation in lieu of the said notice, nor was paid retrenchment compensation, it cannot be said that the 
provisions of Section 25F of the Act were duly complied with. It has been time and again held by this Court as 
well as by the Apex Court that the non-compliance of the mandatory provisions of Section 25F of the Act would 
render the termination of service void ab initio. I am fortified in this view by a decision of the Apex Court in 
the case in Mohanlal v. Management of M/s. Bharat Electronics Ltd., (1981) 3 SCC 225 : (1981) 
Lab IC 806). ” 


8) Gauri Shankar Vs. State of Rajasthan, reported in 2015 LLR 785. In the said case, the Hon’ble Supreme 
Court observed as under :- 


“15. Therefore, in view of the above said case, the learned single Judge in exercise of its powers under 
Articles 226 and 227 of the Constitution of India erroneously interfered with the award of reinstatement and 
future salary from the date of award till date of reinstatement as rightly passed by the Labour Court recording 
valid and cogent reasons in answer to the points of dispute holding that the workman has worked from 
11.1987 to 1.4.1992 and that non-compliance of the mandatory requirements under Sections 25F, 25G and 
25H of the Act by the respondent-Department rendered its action of termination of the services of the workman 
as void ab initio in law and instead the High Court erroneously awarded a compensation of Rs. 1,50,000/- in 
lieu of reinstatement. - - - - - - - - - ri. 


9) Bright Export Limited v/s. Central Board of Trustee, EPF Organisation, reported in 2016 LLR 487. In the 
said case, the Hon’ble Delhi High Court observed as under :- 


“When a particular fact is within the special knowledge of the opposite party, the burden to prove the same lies 
upon the opposite party in terms of section 106 of the Indian Evidence Act, 1872, even if the same has been 
pleaded by other party.” 


10) Sanjay Kumar s/o Surendra Kumar Sharma V/s. Chief Executive Officer, Janpad Panchayat, 
Ratlam, reported in 2010 LLR 1065. In the said case, the Hon’ble Madhya Pradesh High Court observed as under :- 


“If the party possesses best evidence and is not produced before the Court, an adverse inference can be drawn 
under the Evidence Act against such a party.” 


11) Vilas Agaji Pawar and others V/s. The Union of India Additional Solicitor General and Others, Writ 
Petition No. 379 of 2024 dated 12“ January, 2024. In the said case, the Hon’ble Bombay High Court observed as 


596 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : FEBRUARY 7, 2026/MAGHA 18, 1947 [Part II—SEC. 3(ii)] 


under :- 


“19. It is, thus, crystal clear that the OTM was meant only for those Safai Karmacharis or Sub-Staff, who were 
already working with the Bank. These directions of the Honourable Supreme Court read with the law laid 
down in Umadevi (supra), lead to a sine-qua-non that the only option available for the Bank was to ensure 
that the long serving employees as like the Petitioners at it's various establishments and branches in India, 
were considered for the OTM.” 


12) Chief Conservator of Forests, Pune (T) and another, V/s. Janabai Sonaba Sarpale, reported in 2019 (1) 
Bom.LC 18. In the said case, the Hon’ble Bombay High Court has observed as under :- 


“25. The Apex Court in MSRTC (supra) has clearly observed that the issue relating to unfair labour practices 
was neither referred nor considered or decided in Uma Devi's case and therefore, the ruling in., Uma Devi 
(supra) does not denude the Industrial and Labour Courts of their statutory power under section 30 read with 
section 32 of the MRTUP and PULP Act to order permanency of the workers who have been victim of unfair 
labour practice on the part of the employer under Item 6 of Schedule IV. The Apex Court in fact, held that Uma 
Devi (supra) cannot be held to have overridden the powers of the Industrial and Labour Courts in passing 
appropriate order under section 30 of the MRTU and PULP Act, once a case of unfair labour practice on the 
part of the employer under Item 6 of Schedule IV is established.” 


13) K.V. Durga Prasad & Ors. V/s. Sri. Durgamalleshwara Swami Vari Devasthanam Vijayawada & Ors., 
reported in 1996 LLR 329. In the said case, the Hon’ble Andhra Preadesh High Court observed as under :- 


---------- . Thus, I find that the impugned termination orders are in violation of principles of natural 
justice. Without going to the respective merits of the case, I am inclined to allow this writ petition on the 
ground of contravention of principles of natural justice.” 


14) Spentex Industrial Limited V/s. Member, Industrial Court, Nagpur and others, reported in 2011 (131) 
FLR 843. In the said case, the Hon’ble Bombay High Court Bench at Nagpur observed as under :- 


“6.--------- In the instant case, it is pertinent to note that the Labour Court had passed an order on 
09/03/2009 directing the respondent No. 3 to file a reply to the Enquiry Officer's Report. The next two days i.e 
10th and 11th of March, 2009 were holidays and the respondent No. 3 became aware of the order only on 
12/03/2009. But before he could file a reply, the petitioner-Company hurriedly issued the dismissal order on 
13/03/2009, which was served on the respondent No. 3 on 17/03/2009. It is further required to be noted that 
the Complaint was pending and the respondent No. 3 had sought to amend the Complaint so as to incorporate 
a challenge to the dismissal order and had also filed an application for interim relief in respect of the said 
dismissal order. Both the Courts below in the said fact situation were right in coming to the conclusion that the 
petitioner-Company had sought to avoid filing its reply so that a covert action of dismissal could be taken 
against the respondent No. 3. Though, it is debatable whether the principles applicable in respect of Section 33 
of the Industrial Disputes Act, would stricto sensu apply to the proceedings under the M.R.T.U. & P.U.L.P. Act, 
1971, but the least that was expected from the petitioner-Company was that when the parties were before the 
Court and when an application for interim relief was pending, the petitioner-Company ought to have informed 
the Court of the action it proposes to take based on the enquiry officer's report. Having not done so, in my 
view, the Courts below were right in protecting the services of the respondent No. 3 pending the Complaint. 
The facts of the present case are such that the discretion exercised by the Courts below in granting the interim 
relief cannot be faulted with.” 


20. Learned Advocate on behalf of the first party bank has relied upon the case of Secretary, State of Karnataka 
and Ors. V/s. Umadevi and others, reported in AIR 2006 SC 1806. In the said case, the Hon’ble Supreme Court has 
observed as under:- 


-------- . It has also led to persons who get employed, without the following of a regular procedure or 
even through the backdoor or on daily wages, approaching the Courts, seeking directions to make them 
permanent in their posts and to prevent to regular recruitment to the concerned posts. - - - - - - - - - . It is time, 
that Courts desist from issuing orders preventing regular selection or recruitment at the instance of such 
persons and from issuing directions for continuance of those who have not secured regular appointments as per 
procedure established. The passing of orders for continuance, tends to defeat the very Constitutional scheme of 
public employment. - - - - - - - - - ae 


Thus, it becomes clear from the observation of the Hon’ble Supreme Court in the said case that the persons 
who did not enter the services through any open competition or selection process as required by the law, had no right 
to the post held by them or to be continued in service. 


21. Having regard to all the legal as well as factual aspects involved in the matter as discussed herein above, and 
after giving thoughtful consideration to the observations of the Hon’ble Supreme Court and the Hon’ble High Courts 
in the above referred case laws, this Court is of the view that the provisions of the Industrial Dispute Acts are 
applicable to the first party, and the second party has completed 240 days in the preceding 12 months of his oral 


[भाग 1---खण्ड 3(ii)] भारत का राजपत्र : फरवरी 7, 2026/माध 18, 1947 597 


termination dated 01.02.2016, and his termination is without following mandatory provisions of the Industrial 
Disputes Act, 1947, which is illegal and improper termination violating the statutory provisions of the Industrial 
Disputes Act, 1947. Resultantly, issue No. | is answered affirmatively. 


22. Considering the entire factual and admitted position of the present case as well as the provisions of law, 
reinstatement of the second party workman with the first party will not be appropriate. Instead of reinstatement, 
awarding a reasonable lump-sum amount of compensation to the second party workman would be just and 
appropriate. In view of the facts and circumstances of the case, taking into consideration last designation, last drawn 
wages, service tenure and age of the second party workman, in my considered view, awarding lump-sum 
compensation amount of Rs. 50,000/- to the second party workman would meet the ends of justice. Resultantly, issue 
No. 2 is answered accordingly. For issue No. 3, the following Award is passed. 


AWARD 
1. The reference is answered partly affirmative as under :- 
A It is declared that the oral termination of the second party dated 01.02.2016 is illegal and improper. 
B. The second party workman is entitled for lump-sum compensation amount of Rs. 50,000/-. 
C. The first party is directed to pay compensation amount of Rs. 50,000/- to the second party workman. 


D. The first party is hereby directed to comply the order within two months from the date of publication of the 
Award. 


E. If the first party failed to comply the order within time, then the second party workman is entitled to simple 
interest at the rate of 6% per annum on the compensation amount of Rs. 50,000/-, from the date of this Award till 
its actual realization. 


3. Five copies of this Award be sent to Regional Labour Commissioner, office of the Regional Labour 
Commissioner, 1, Kaul Building, Shankar Seth Road, Gururnanak Nagar, Pune — 411002 for publication and 
further necessary action. 


4. No order as to costs.
